A nonlocal West Nile virus model with nonlocal free boundary conditions driven by both mosquitoes and birds
Authors: Xin Long, Yijun Lou, Wenjie Ni, Taishan Yi
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
This paper presents a novel West Nile virus model that has more extensive free boundary conditions and also takes into account the impact of infected mosquitoes on the free boundary, both of which are firsts in West Nile virus modeling. Specifically, the free boundary conditions independent of the dispersal kernel functions in the equations, bring new challenges to the dynamical analysis of spreading-vanishing, especially for the case where the basic reproduction number \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal R_0\le 1$$\end{document}R0≤1, which involves new ideas and techniques for dynamics analysis. Moreover, due to the consideration of the impact of infected mosquitoes in the free boundary conditions, new conclusions have been obtained. Numerical schemes have been developed, which not only verify qualitative theoretical results, but also provide novel quantitative insights into the effects of various factors on transmission dynamics. Overall, our results not only differ significantly from the local diffusion version presented in Lin and Zhu (2017) but also extend all the conclusions from the nonlocal diffusion version in Du and Ni (2020), with some conclusions obtained under more general conditions.

Introduction

West Nile virus (WNv) is a mosquito-borne virus of the Flaviviridae family, which was first isolated from the blood of a febrile woman in the West Nile province of Uganda in 1937 (Abdelrazec et al. 2014; Bowman et al. 2005), and first brought a major outbreak in New York City in late summer of 1999 and spread rapidly across North America (Thomas and Urena 2001; Lin and Zhu 2017; Moschini et al. 2017; Ben-Nathan et al. 2006; Abdelrazec et al. 2014; Wonham et al. 2004). The transmission of the virus circulates between mosquitoes and birds, with humans and other vertebrates being incidental hosts (Ben-Nathan et al. 2006; Abdelrazec et al. 2014; Wonham et al. 2004). It’s worth noting that the virus is transmitted to humans by mosquitoes, but cannot be transmitted back to mosquitoes (Thomas and Urena 2001; Abdelrazec et al. 2014). Therefore, humans are a dead-end host in the life cycle of the virus. Unfortunately, humans exhibit adverse effects from the virus (Bowman et al. 2005). The virus can cause inflammation of the brain and usually results in flu-like symptoms such as fever, vomiting, and stiffness of the neck (Ceausu et al. 1997; Bowman et al. 2005). The disease can lead to severe symptoms, including altered consciousness, extremity tremors, paralysis, and death (Tsai et al. 1998; Bowman et al. 2005). Since 1999, there have been more than 18,698 reported cases of WNv in humans, with more than 715 deaths (Ben-Nathan et al. 2006). There is no specific treatment available for the virus and therefore, control of the disease depends on breaking the transmission cycle of the virus (Thomas and Urena 2001; Bowman et al. 2005). These findings underscore the importance of understanding the transmission mechanisms of WNv (Lin and Zhu 2017; Thomas and Urena 2001).

To the best of our knowledge, the first model of WNv was proposed in Thomas and Urena (2001), which is a difference equation model. Subsequently, studies (Wonham et al. 2004; Bowman et al. 2005; Abdelrazec et al. 2014) used systems of ordinary differential equations (ODEs) to model the spread of WNv in mosquito and bird populations. To describe the spatio-temporal transmission dynamics, random diffusion terms are included in the models to approximate the movement of birds and mosquitoes. For instance, the study in Lewis et al. (2006) proposed the following simplified model for WNv:1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} B_t=d_1 B_{xx}+a_1(e_1-B)M-b_1B,&\ x\in \mathbb {R}, \ t>0,\\ M_t=d_2 M_{xx}+a_2(e_2-M)B-b_2M,&\ x\in \mathbb {R}, \ t>0, \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Bt=d1Bxx+a1(e1-B)M-b1B,x∈R,t>0,Mt=d2Mxx+a2(e2-M)B-b2M,x∈R,t>0,where2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} a_1=\frac{\alpha _B\beta _R}{e_1} \quad \text {and} \quad a_2=\frac{\alpha _M\beta _R}{e_1}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}a1=αBβRe1anda2=αMβRe1,B(x, t) and M(x, t) are the densities of the infected bird (host) and mosquito (vector) populations at position x and time t, respectively. The parameters are all positive constants: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_1$$\end{document}d1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_2$$\end{document}d2 are the diffusion rates of birds and mosquitoes respectively; \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_1$$\end{document}b1 is the combination of two rates, reversion rate from infectivity to susceptibility and natural death rate of birds; \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_2$$\end{document}b2 is the mosquito death rate; \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_1$$\end{document}e1 is the total number of susceptible and infected birds, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_2$$\end{document}e2 is the total number of susceptible and infected mosquitoes; \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _B$$\end{document}αB, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _M$$\end{document}αM respectively stand for the WNv transmission probability per bite to birds and mosquitoes; \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta _R$$\end{document}βR is the per-capita biting rate of mosquitoes on birds. The model is based on ideal assumptions such as that infection does not confer immunity to birds, we refer to Lewis et al. (2006) and Lin and Zhu (2017) for further details for model formulation.

Please note that the ODE version of (1) is widely known as the classical Ross-Macdonald model (Wang et al. 2020), which was formulated to describe the transmission of malaria, and is widely used to describe many other mosquito-borne diseases. Therefore, mathematical results for (1) and its variations discussed in this study are also applicable to the spread of other mosquito borne infections. A key index to characterize the transmission dynamics of (1) is the basic reproduction number, given by3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} The main results of Lewis et al. Although the asymptotic wave speed can approximately estimate the progressive spreading speed of the virus transmission, it does not truly reflect the spread of the virus in the early stage of the infection’s spatial expansion to a larger area. Using a free boundary to model the spatial spread of the virus can better reflect this situation. That is to say, at the forefront of the boundary of the infected area, the virus expands and moves forward, thereby triggering further spatial propagation, until the entire region or area becomes endemic. Recently, Lin and Zhu (2017), inspired by the work in Du and Lin (2010), studied the following WNv model with local diffusion and free boundaries:5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} Recently, Wang et al. (2019) estimated the spreading speed of (5) when spreading happens.

Noting that in both (1) and (5), the spatial movement of individuals is described by the local diffusion term \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$du_{xx}$$\end{document}duxx, which fails to account for nonlocal factors such as long-distance dispersal that are commonly observed in many real-world propagation processes (Clobert et al. 2012; Nathan et al. 2012). To incorporate such nonlocal effects, one approach is to replace \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$du_{xx}$$\end{document}duxx with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d\Big [\displaystyle \int _{\mathbb R}J(x-y)u(t,y){\textrm{d}}y-u(t,x)\Big ]$$\end{document}d[∫RJ(x-y)u(t,y)dy-u(t,x)]. This modification implies that an individual located at x can migrate to any other location y with a probability given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J(x-y)$$\end{document}J(x-y), and the migration frequency is d per unit time. In recent years, nonlocal counterparts of (1) have attracted extensive attention (see Bao et al. (2016); Bao and Shen (2017); Berestycki et al. (2016a, 2016b); Garnier (2011); Hutson et al. (2003); Li et al. (2010) and references therein). \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{J})$$\end{document}(J):\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} The results in Du and Ni (2020) show that if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{J})$$\end{document}(J) and (8) hold, then problem (7) admits a unique positive solution (B, M, g, h) defined for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t>0$$\end{document}t>0 and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\{ \begin{array}{l} \text{ if } ~\mathcal R_0\le 1, \mathrm{ then } {\boldsymbol{Vanishing}} happens: \\ \ \ \ ~h_\infty -g_\infty <\infty ~and \lim \limits _{t\rightarrow \infty }(\Vert B(\cdot ,t) \Vert _{C([g(t),h(t)])} +\Vert M(\cdot ,t)\Vert _{C([g(t),h(t)])})=0; Different from random diffusion model (5), the nonlocal diffusion variation (7) leads to the emergence of accelerated diffusion phenomenon. In addition, Du and Ni (2022) also presented the estimation of the finite propagation speed.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_i\in C({\mathbb R})\cap L^\infty ({\mathbb R})$$\end{document}Ji∈C(R)∩L∞(R) is nonnegative, symmetric, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_i(0)>0$$\end{document}Ji(0)>0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\displaystyle \int _{\mathbb R}J_i(x) \textrm{d}x=1$$\end{document}∫RJi(x)dx=1, i=1,2.

It is not difficult to find that if we define9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} K_{J_1}(x):=\int _{x}^{+\infty } J_1(y) \textrm{d} y, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}KJ1(x):=∫x+∞J1(y)dy,then the free boundary conditions in (7) can be written equivalently as10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \displaystyle h^{\prime }(t) =\mu F_{h, K_{J_1}}:=\mu \int _{g(t)}^{h(t)} B(t, x) K_{J_1}(h(t)-x) \textrm{d} x,\\ \displaystyle g'(t) =-\mu F_{g, K_{J_1}}:=-\mu \int _{g(t)}^{h(t)} B(t, x) K_{J_1}(x-g(t)) \textrm{d} x. \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}h′(t)=μFh,KJ1:=μ∫g(t)h(t)B(t,x)KJ1(h(t)-x)dx,g′(t)=-μFg,KJ1:=-μ∫g(t)h(t)B(t,x)KJ1(x-g(t))dx.As shown in Cao et al. (2019), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_{h, K_{J_1}}$$\end{document}Fh,KJ1 is the outward flux of a population at free boundary h(t), that is, the total population mass moved out of the range [g(t), h(t)] at time t through its right boundary \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x = h(t)$$\end{document}x=h(t) per unit time. Furthermore, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h^{\prime }(t) =\mu F_{h, K_{J_1}}$$\end{document}h′(t)=μFh,KJ1 can be interpreted as assuming that the expanding rate of the front is proportional to the outward flux. And \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_{g, K_{J_1}}$$\end{document}Fg,KJ1 can also be explained in a similar manner. Obviously, (10) indicates that the outward flux is closely dependent on the diffusion kernel function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_1$$\end{document}J1, and the pushing of the free boundary is only influenced by infected birds.

A natural question arises: what happens when the outward flux is determined by a general function K instead of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_{J_1}$$\end{document}KJ1? In fact, a recent study (Long et al. 2024) considered this issue and proposed the following new free boundary conditions:11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \displaystyle h^{\prime }(t) =\mu F_{h, K}:=\mu \int _{g(t)}^{h(t)} B(t, x) K(h(t)-x) \textrm{d} x,\\ \displaystyle g'(t) =-\mu F_{g, K}:=-\mu \int _{g(t)}^{h(t)} B(t, x) K(x-g(t)) \textrm{d} x, \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}h′(t)=μFh,K:=μ∫g(t)h(t)B(t,x)K(h(t)-x)dx,g′(t)=-μFg,K:=-μ∫g(t)h(t)B(t,x)K(x-g(t))dx,which indicates that the contribution of each point in [g(t), h(t)] to the outward flux at h(t) is closely related to a general weighting function K of the distance from that point to the boundary h(t). This represents a very different assumption that the movement of species boundaries can be independent of their dispersal strategies. Such a function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K(h(t)-x)$$\end{document}K(h(t)-x) has also been applied in bird flight models to describe that the movement of individuals within a population is restricted by their distance from the boundary (Nagy et al. 2010). For example, K may be chosen as a combination of certain exponential functions (Feng et al. 2022). In general, we can find that the free boundary conditions in some existing studies (Cao et al. (2019); Du et al. (2021, 2022); Lin and Zhu (2017); Wang et al. (2019); Du and Ni (2020, 2022); Du et al. (2024)) are a special case of (11), which represents a more extensive free boundary condition.

On the other hand, both (5) and (7) assume that the expansion speed of the infection boundary front is only driven by the infected birds (Du and Ni 2020; Lin and Zhu 2017). This is because they assumed the impact of mosquitoes on the progression of the infection boundary front to be negligible. However, since the number of mosquitoes far exceeds that of birds and mosquitoes move freely, they may have a significant capacity to spread viruses and push the free boundary. Therefore, it would be more reasonable to consider that both infected mosquitoes and birds play a role in advancing the infection frontier.

Inspired by the above discussion, in this paper, we study the following model:12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \displaystyle B_t=d_1 \int _{g(t)}^{h(t)}J_1(x-y)B(t,y)\textrm{d}y-d_1 B(t,x) \\ ~~~~~~~~~~~~+a_1(e_1-B(t,x))M(t,x)-b_1 B(t,x), & t>0,\ x\in (g(t), h(t)),\\ \displaystyle M_t=d_2 \int _{g(t)}^{h(t)}J_2(x-y)M(t,y)\textrm{d}y-d_2 M(t,x) \\ ~~~~~~~~~~~~+a_2(e_2-M(t,x))B(t,x)-b_2 M(t,x) , & t>0,\ x\in (g(t), h(t)),\\ \displaystyle g'(t)= -\mu _1 \int _{g(t)}^{h(t)}K_1(x-g(t))B(t,x)\textrm{d}x \\ ~~~~~~~~~~~~ \displaystyle -\mu _2 \int _{g(t)}^{h(t)}K_2(x-g(t))M(t,x)\textrm{d}x, & t>0,\\ \displaystyle h'(t)= \mu _1 \int _{g(t)}^{h(t)}K_1(h(t)-x)B(t,x)\textrm{d}x \\ ~~~~~~~~~~~~ \displaystyle + \mu _2 \int _{g(t)}^{h(t)}K_2(h(t)-x)M(t,x)\textrm{d}x, & t>0,\\ B(t,x)= M(t,x)=0, & t >0, \; x\in \{g(t), h(t)\},\\ B(0,x)=u_{10}(x),\ M(0,x)=u_{20}(x), \ & x\in [-h_0,h_0], \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Bt=d1∫g(t)h(t)J1(x-y)B(t,y)dy-d1B(t,x)+a1(e1-B(t,x))M(t,x)-b1B(t,x),t>0,x∈(g(t),h(t)),Mt=d2∫g(t)h(t)J2(x-y)M(t,y)dy-d2M(t,x)+a2(e2-M(t,x))B(t,x)-b2M(t,x),t>0,x∈(g(t),h(t)),g′(t)=-μ1∫g(t)h(t)K1(x-g(t))B(t,x)dx-μ2∫g(t)h(t)K2(x-g(t))M(t,x)dx,t>0,h′(t)=μ1∫g(t)h(t)K1(h(t)-x)B(t,x)dx+μ2∫g(t)h(t)K2(h(t)-x)M(t,x)dx,t>0,B(t,x)=M(t,x)=0,t>0,x∈{g(t),h(t)},B(0,x)=u10(x),M(0,x)=u20(x),x∈[-h0,h0],where constants \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _1,\mu _2>0$$\end{document}μ1,μ2>0 and the initial functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{i0}(x)$$\end{document}ui0(x) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(i=1,2)$$\end{document}(i=1,2) satisfy (8). We assume infection does not confer immunity to birds, the kernel functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_i:{\mathbb R}\rightarrow {\mathbb R}$$\end{document}Ji:R→R \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(i=1,2)$$\end{document}(i=1,2) satisfy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_i(x)$$\end{document}Ki(x) are nonnegative and locally Lipschitz continuous in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[0,\infty )$$\end{document}[0,∞), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_i (0)>0 $$\end{document}Ki(0)>0.

For any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y\in [0,\infty )$$\end{document}y∈[0,∞), there exist constants \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa _i>0$$\end{document}κi>0 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\displaystyle \int _{y}^{\infty } J_i(x) {\textrm{d}}x\ge \kappa _i K_i(y)$$\end{document}∫y∞Ji(x)dx≥κiKi(y), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i=1,2$$\end{document}i=1,2.

The main results in this study can be summarized in the following three theorems.

Theorem 1.1

(Existence and uniqueness) Assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{J})$$\end{document}(J) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{K})$$\end{document}(K) hold. Then for any given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_0>0$$\end{document}h0>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u_{10},u_{20} )$$\end{document}(u10,u20) satisfy (8), problem (12) admits a unique positive solution (B, M, g, h) defined for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t>0$$\end{document}t>0.

Theorem 1.2

(Spreading-vanishing dichotomy) Assume \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{J})$$\end{document}(J), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{K})$$\end{document}(K) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{K1})$$\end{document}(K1) hold, and the initial functions satisfy (8). Let (B, M, g, h) be the solution of (12), and denote13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} g_\infty :=\lim \limits _{t\rightarrow \infty }g(t) \ \ \ \ \textrm{and} \ \ \ \ h_\infty :=\lim \limits _{t\rightarrow \infty }h(t). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}g∞:=limt→∞g(t)andh∞:=limt→∞h(t).Then one of the following alternatives must occur: (i)Spreading: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-g_\infty =h_\infty =\infty $$\end{document}-g∞=h∞=∞ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim \limits _{t\rightarrow \infty }(B(t,x), M(t,x))=(B^*,M^*) \text{ locally } \text{ uniformly } \text{ in } {\mathbb R}; \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limt→∞(B(t,x),M(t,x))=(B∗,M∗)locallyuniformlyinR;(ii)Vanishing: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_\infty -g_\infty <\infty $$\end{document}h∞-g∞<∞ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \lim \limits _{t\rightarrow \infty }(B(t,x), M(t,x))=(0,0) \text{ uniformly } \text{ for } x\in [g(t),h(t)]. $$\end{document}limt→∞(B(t,x),M(t,x))=(0,0)uniformlyforx∈[g(t),h(t)].

(i)Spreading: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-g_\infty =h_\infty =\infty $$\end{document}-g∞=h∞=∞ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim \limits _{t\rightarrow \infty }(B(t,x), M(t,x))=(B^*,M^*) \text{ locally } \text{ uniformly } \text{ in } {\mathbb R}; \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limt→∞(B(t,x),M(t,x))=(B∗,M∗)locallyuniformlyinR;

Spreading: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-g_\infty =h_\infty =\infty $$\end{document}-g∞=h∞=∞ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim \limits _{t\rightarrow \infty }(B(t,x), M(t,x))=(B^*,M^*) \text{ locally } \text{ uniformly } \text{ in } {\mathbb R}; \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limt→∞(B(t,x),M(t,x))=(B∗,M∗)locallyuniformlyinR;

Vanishing: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_\infty -g_\infty <\infty $$\end{document}h∞-g∞<∞ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \lim \limits _{t\rightarrow \infty }(B(t,x), M(t,x))=(0,0) \text{ uniformly } \text{ for } x\in [g(t),h(t)]. $$\end{document}limt→∞(B(t,x),M(t,x))=(0,0)uniformlyforx∈[g(t),h(t)].

Remark 1.4

In fact, for the existing free boundary conditions considered in Cao et al. (2019) and Du and Ni (2020), condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{K1})$$\end{document}(K1) is automatically satisfied, as in these models the boundary kernel is given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_i(x) = \int _x^{+\infty } J_i(y)\,dy$$\end{document}Ki(x)=∫x+∞Ji(y)dy. This observation indicates that condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{K1})$$\end{document}(K1) serves as a natural generalization of the assumption adopted in the earlier models.

From a biological perspective, the boundary kernel function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_i$$\end{document}Ki characterizes the capacity of a population to expand outward at the moving boundary. In addition to the dispersal strategy within the habitat, as described by the kernel \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_i$$\end{document}Ji, this expansion capacity is also modulated by external factors such as environmental resistance and natural predation. Condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{K1})$$\end{document}(K1) thus imposes a biologically reasonable constraint that links the mechanism of boundary expansion to the internal dispersal process: although the expansion capacity at the boundary may differ from the dispersal strategy within the habitat, it remains regulated by the latter and cannot exceed the dispersal kernel functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_i$$\end{document}Ji by an unreasonable margin.

Before moving to the theoretical proofs for these results in the next section, we would like to briefly highlight main contributions of the current study.

Remark 1.5

Some remarks on the current study: (i)[Model formulation:] Model (12) incorporates more extensive free boundary conditions independent of dispersal kernel functions and includes the impact of infected mosquitoes on boundary expansion. To our knowledge, these aspects are novel in the WNv modeling.(ii)[Theoretical challenges:] The introduction of new free boundary conditions, independent of the dispersal kernel \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_i$$\end{document}Ji, involves novel ideas and techniques for dynamics analysis. Our conclusions extend those in Du and Ni (2020), as shown in Theorem 1.3-(i,iii,iv), to general conditions of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ K_i $$\end{document}Ki. The introduction of the parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _2$$\end{document}μ2 also leads to new findings.(iv)[Distinct results:] Compared to the local diffusion model (5), the nonlocal diffusion model (12) yields distinct results. For example, Theorem 1.3-(iii) shows that spreading occurs regardless of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ h_0 $$\end{document}h0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {R}_0 > 1$$\end{document}R0>1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_1, d_2 \ll 1$$\end{document}d1,d2≪1, contrasting sharply with (5) (see Lin and Zhu 2017, Theorem 5.5).(v)[Numerical schemes:] We developed numerical schemes to solve this nonlocal diffusion model with free boundaries defined by nonlocal integrals. (v)[Numerical schemes:] We developed numerical schemes to solve this nonlocal diffusion model with free boundaries defined by nonlocal integrals. To our knowledge, this appears to be the first numerical work for such extensive free boundary conditions, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_i(i=1,2)$$\end{document}Ki(i=1,2) is merely a general non negative locally Lipschitz continuous function.

[Numerical schemes:] We developed numerical schemes to solve this nonlocal diffusion model with free boundaries defined by nonlocal integrals. Simulations verify the reliability of our conclusions and provide quantitative insights. To our knowledge, this appears to be the first numerical work for such extensive free boundary conditions, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_i(i=1,2)$$\end{document}Ki(i=1,2) is merely a general non negative locally Lipschitz continuous function.

As in some existing studies (Du et al. (2021); Du and Ni (2022); Wang et al. (2019)), when propagation occurs, one naturally wonders what would be the spreading speed. This question will be explored in a subsequent paper. The rest of this paper is organised as follows. Section 2 introduces some notations and presents preliminary results on the comparison principle, the associated eigenvalue problem, and the fixed boundary problem, which are crucial for our later analysis. Section 3 establishes the global existence and uniqueness of solutions for a more general system, of which both (7) and (12) are a special case. The conditions needed are weaker than those in Du et al. (2022). Section 4 is dedicated to proving Theorems 1.2 and 1.3, which extensively utilizes the results from Sect. 2, and requires new ideas and techniques to address the challenges posed by the new free boundary conditions independent of J. This is particularly evident in addressing the scenario where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {R}_0 \le 1$$\end{document}R0≤1 and managing the introduction of the new parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _2$$\end{document}μ2. Sect. 5 focuses on numerical simulations to verify theoretical results and provide additional quantitative insights.

Lemma 2.1

Remark 2.2

When g(t) and h(t) are constants, the conclusion of Lemma 2.1 still holds without requiring the second line of (15). For details, see Lemma 3.1 in Du and Ni (2020).

For any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L>0$$\end{document}L>0, the following results hold for the associated eigenvalue problem:16\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \displaystyle -d_1 \int _{-L}^{L}J_1(x-y)\phi (y)\textrm{d}y-d_1 \phi (x) =a_1e_1\psi -b_1 \phi +\lambda \phi , & x\in [-L, L],\\ \displaystyle -d_2 \int _{-L}^{L}J_2(x-y)\psi (y)\textrm{d}y-d_2 \psi (x)=a_2e_2\phi -b_2 \psi +\lambda \psi , & x\in [-L, L]. \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}-d1∫-LLJ1(x-y)ϕ(y)dy-d1ϕ(x)=a1e1ψ-b1ϕ+λϕ,x∈[-L,L],-d2∫-LLJ2(x-y)ψ(y)dy-d2ψ(x)=a2e2ϕ-b2ψ+λψ,x∈[-L,L].

Global existence and uniqueness of solutions

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(t,x,u_1,u_2)\in {\mathbb R}^+\times {\mathbb R}\times [0,\rho _1]\times [0,\rho _2]$$\end{document}(t,x,u1,u2)∈R+×R×[0,ρ1]×[0,ρ2]. It is easy to see that nonlinear functions in (12) are given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f_1(u_1,u_2)=a_1(e_1-u_1)u_2-b_1u_1,\ \ f_2(u_1,u_2)=a_2(e_2-u_2)u_1-b_2 u_2, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f1(u1,u2)=a1(e1-u1)u2-b1u1,f2(u1,u2)=a2(e2-u2)u1-b2u2,which satisfy the conditions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbf {(F),(F1)}$$\end{document}(F),(F1) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mathbf {(F2)}$$\end{document}(F2). In light of the inequalities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_1(t, x, 0, u_2) > 0$$\end{document}f1(t,x,0,u2)>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_2(t, x, u_1, 0) > 0$$\end{document}f2(t,x,u1,0)>0 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_1, u_2 > 0$$\end{document}u1,u2>0, it is evident that these functions do not satisfy the condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbf {(f)}$$\end{document}(f) in Du et al. (2022). Consequently, the conditions considered in this study are less restrictive than those presented in Du et al. (2022).

In view of Lemma 4.2 in Du and Ni (2020), we can directly obtain the following conclusions, which will be used in the proof of Theorem 1.1.

Theorem 3.4

Proof

Thanks to Lemmas 3.2 and 3.3, we obtain that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma $$\end{document}Γ has a unique fixed point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(g_*,h_*)$$\end{document}(g∗,h∗) in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \Sigma _{c_0,s,\epsilon _0}$$\end{document}Σc0,s,ϵ0 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s\in (0, T_*]$$\end{document}s∈(0,T∗] by using the contraction mapping theorem, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_0$$\end{document}c0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_*$$\end{document}T∗, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon _0$$\end{document}ϵ0 come from Lemmas 3.2 and 3.3. Therefore, it follows from (22) and Lemma 3.1 that (19) has a unique solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u_{1*}, u_{2*}, g_*,h_*)$$\end{document}(u1∗,u2∗,g∗,h∗) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in [0, T_*]$$\end{document}t∈[0,T∗]. By using the extension arguments, we can show that the solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u_{1*}, u_{2*}, g_*,h_*)$$\end{document}(u1∗,u2∗,g∗,h∗) on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[0, T_*]$$\end{document}[0,T∗] can be extended to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(0,\infty )$$\end{document}(0,∞). We omit the details here due to the proof is almost identical to the fourth step of Theorem 2.1 in Du et al. (2022). \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Since the unique solution of (12) depends continuously on the parameters in (12), the desired result then follows by letting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}ϵ→0. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

To emphasize the dependence on the parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _1$$\end{document}μ1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _2$$\end{document}μ2, we denote the solution of problem (12) as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( B_{\mu _1,\mu _2}, M_{\mu _1,\mu _2}, g_{\mu _1,\mu _2}, h_{\mu _1,\mu _2}\right) $$\end{document}Bμ1,μ2,Mμ1,μ2,gμ1,μ2,hμ1,μ2. The following result directly follows from Lemma 3.5.

Corollary 3.6

Suppose that (J) and (K) hold. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _1\le \mu _3$$\end{document}μ1≤μ3 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _2\le \mu _4$$\end{document}μ2≤μ4, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_{\mu _1,\mu _2} (t)\le h_{\mu _3,\mu _4} (t)$$\end{document}hμ1,μ2(t)≤hμ3,μ4(t) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_{\mu _1,\mu _2} (t)\ge g_{\mu _3,\mu _4} (t)$$\end{document}gμ1,μ2(t)≥gμ3,μ4(t) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t>0$$\end{document}t>0. Moreover, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_{\mu _1,\mu _2}(t,x)\le B_{\mu _3,\mu _4}(t,x)$$\end{document}Bμ1,μ2(t,x)≤Bμ3,μ4(t,x), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{\mu _1,\mu _2}(t,x)\le M_{\mu _3,\mu _4}(t,x)$$\end{document}Mμ1,μ2(t,x)≤Mμ3,μ4(t,x) for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t>0$$\end{document}t>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_{\mu _1,\mu _2} (t)<x<h_{\mu _1,\mu _2} (t)$$\end{document}gμ1,μ2(t)<x<hμ1,μ2(t).

Spreading-vanishing dichotomy and criteria

We will prove Theorems 1.2 and 1.3 in this section. Lemma 4.3

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {R}_0= 1$$\end{document}R0=1 and (K1) holds, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_\infty -g_\infty <\infty $$\end{document}h∞-g∞<∞ and vanishing occurs.

Proof

We only need to show \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_\infty -g_\infty <\infty $$\end{document}h∞-g∞<∞ due to Lemma 4.1. \textrm{d}x\\ \le&\;-\sigma [h'(t)-g'(t)]. a result, for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t> 0$$\end{document}t>0 we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_{\infty }-g_{\infty }<\infty $$\end{document}h∞-g∞<∞. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Remark 4.4

It is not difficult to find that the proof of Lemma 4.3 is also valid for the case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal R_0<1$$\end{document}R0<1. Moreover, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_i$$\end{document}Ki (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i=1,2$$\end{document}i=1,2) satisfy condition (K1), then by (J) it follows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_i\in L^\infty ([0,\infty ))$$\end{document}Ki∈L∞([0,∞)), which implies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\limsup \limits _{y\rightarrow \infty }\frac{\int _0^y K_i(x)\textrm{d}x}{y}<\infty ~(i=1,2)$$\end{document}lim supy→∞∫0yKi(x)dxy<∞(i=1,2). Therefore, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {R}_0\le 1$$\end{document}R0≤1 and (K1) holds, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_\infty -g_\infty <\infty $$\end{document}h∞-g∞<∞ and vanishing occurs.

Remark 4.5

To address the difficulty arising from the independence of the boundary kernel function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_i$$\end{document}Ki and the dispersal kernel function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_i$$\end{document}Ji, we adopt some new ideas and techniques in our analysis. In particular, under the assumption \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal R_0<1$$\end{document}R0<1 (see Lemma 4.2), a new pair of upper solutions is constructed, which differs substantially from the approaches used in Lin and Zhu (2017) and Du and Ni (2020). For the critical case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {R}_0 = 1$$\end{document}R0=1, condition (K1) is imposed in Lemma 4.3, revealing a subtle interplay between the dispersal condition and the free boundary condition.

Lemma 4.6

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1(-h_0,h_0)$$\end{document}λ1(-h0,h0) be the principal eigenvalue of (16) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L=h_0$$\end{document}L=h0. Remark 4.7

Lemma 4.8

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1(g(t_0), h(t_0))<0$$\end{document}λ1(g(t0),h(t0))<0 for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_0\ge 0$$\end{document}t0≥0, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-g_\infty =h_\infty =+\infty $$\end{document}-g∞=h∞=+∞. Further assuming that (K1) holds or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal { R}_0>1$$\end{document}R0>1, we can obtain35\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{t\rightarrow \infty }M(t,x)=M^*,\ \ \lim _{t\rightarrow \infty }B(t,x)=B^*\ \ \ \ \mathrm{locally\ uniformly\ in}\ {\mathbb R}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limt→∞M(t,x)=M∗,limt→∞B(t,x)=B∗locallyuniformlyinR,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1(g(t_0), h(t_0))$$\end{document}λ1(g(t0),h(t0)) is the eigenvalue of (16) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[-L,L]$$\end{document}[-L,L] replaced by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[g(t_0), h(t_0)]$$\end{document}[g(t0),h(t0)].

Proof

We first claim that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_\infty -g_\infty =\infty $$\end{document}h∞-g∞=∞. Suppose, on the contrary, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_\infty -g_\infty <\infty $$\end{document}h∞-g∞<∞. Then, by Lemma 4.1, we deduce\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \lambda _1(g_\infty ,h_\infty )\ge 0, $$\end{document}λ1(g∞,h∞)≥0,which together with Proposition 2.4-(i) yields that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lambda _1(g(t),h(t))\ge 0 \ \ \ \textrm{for}\ t\ge 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}λ1(g(t),h(t))≥0fort≥0.However, this leads to a contradiction to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1(g(t_0), h(t_0))<0$$\end{document}λ1(g(t0),h(t0))<0 for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_0\ge 0$$\end{document}t0≥0.

Next, we prove that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-g_\infty =h_\infty =+\infty $$\end{document}-g∞=h∞=+∞. Without loss of generality, we assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_\infty <+\infty $$\end{document}h∞<+∞ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_\infty =-\infty $$\end{document}g∞=-∞. Hence, as in the proof of Lemma 4.1, by making use of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_\infty <+\infty $$\end{document}h∞<+∞ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1(g(t_0), h(t_0))<0$$\end{document}λ1(g(t0),h(t0))<0 for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_0\ge 0$$\end{document}t0≥0, we can find some constants \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta >0$$\end{document}δ>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_1>t_0$$\end{document}T1>t0 such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \displaystyle h'(t)&= \mu _1 \int _{g(t)}^{h(t)}K_1(h(t)-x)B(t,x)\textrm{d}x +\mu _2 \int _{g(t)}^{h(t)}K_2(h(t)-x)M(t,x)\textrm{d}x\ge \delta >0\\ \end{aligned}$$\end{document}h′(t)=μ1∫g(t)h(t)K1(h(t)-x)B(t,x)dx+μ2∫g(t)h(t)K2(h(t)-x)M(t,x)dx≥δ>0 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t>T_1$$\end{document}t>T1. However, this leads to a contradiction to the fact \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_\infty <\infty $$\end{document}h∞<∞.

Finally, we show (35) holds. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\overline{B}(t),\overline{M}(t))$$\end{document}(B¯(t),M¯(t)) be the unique positive solution of the following ordinary differential equations:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \displaystyle \overline{B}_t= a_1(e_1- \overline{B})\overline{M}-b_1 \overline{B}, \ \ \ \ & t>0,\\ \displaystyle \overline{M}_t= a_2(e_2-\overline{M})\overline{B}-b_2\overline{M}, \ \ \ \ & t>0,\\ \overline{B}(0)= e_1,\ \overline{M}(0)= e_2. \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}B¯t=a1(e1-B¯)M¯-b1B¯,t>0,M¯t=a2(e2-M¯)B¯-b2M¯,t>0,B¯(0)=e1,M¯(0)=e2.Then, by Lemma 2.1, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} M(t,x)\le \overline{M}(t),\ \ \ B(t,x)\le \overline{B}(t)\ \ \ \ \ \textrm{for}\ t>0, \; x\in [g(t),h(t)]. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}M(t,x)≤M¯(t),B(t,x)≤B¯(t)fort>0,x∈[g(t),h(t)].Note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_\infty -g_\infty =\infty $$\end{document}h∞-g∞=∞ and (K1) holds. Based on Remark 4.4, we obtain that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal { R}_0>1$$\end{document}R0>1. Hence,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{t\rightarrow \infty }\overline{B}(t)=B^*,\ \ \ \lim _{t\rightarrow \infty }\overline{M}(t)=M^*, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limt→∞B¯(t)=B∗,limt→∞M¯(t)=M∗,which implies36\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} B^*\ge \limsup _{t\rightarrow \infty } B(t,x),\ \ M^* \ge \limsup _{t\rightarrow \infty } M(t,x)&\;\;\; \mathrm{locally\ uniformly\ in}\ {\mathbb R}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}B∗≥lim supt→∞B(t,x),M∗≥lim supt→∞M(t,x)locallyuniformlyinR.On the other hand, for fixed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s\ge t_0$$\end{document}s≥t0, we define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(B_s(t,x),M_s(t,x))$$\end{document}(Bs(t,x),Ms(t,x)) to be a solution of (17) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_L$$\end{document}QL replaced by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(0,\infty )\times [g(s), h(s)] $$\end{document}(0,∞)×[g(s),h(s)], and initial functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_s(0,x)=B(s,x)$$\end{document}Bs(0,x)=B(s,x) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_s(0,x)=M(s,x)$$\end{document}Ms(0,x)=M(s,x). Noting that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1(g(s),h(s))<0$$\end{document}λ1(g(s),h(s))<0, by applying Lemma 2.1 and Proposition 2.5-(iii), we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&0<\widetilde{M}_s(x):=\lim _{t\rightarrow \infty } M_s(t,x)\le \liminf _{t\rightarrow \infty } M(t+s,x) & \mathrm{uniformly\ in}\ [g(s),h(s)],\\&0<\widetilde{B}_s(x):=\lim _{t\rightarrow \infty } B_s(t,x)\le \liminf _{t\rightarrow \infty } B(t+s,x) & \mathrm{uniformly\ in}\ [g(s),h(s)], \end{aligned}$$\end{document}0<M~s(x):=limt→∞Ms(t,x)≤lim inft→∞M(t+s,x)uniformlyin[g(s),h(s)],0<B~s(x):=limt→∞Bs(t,x)≤lim inft→∞B(t+s,x)uniformlyin[g(s),h(s)],where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\widetilde{M}_s(x),\widetilde{B}_s(x))$$\end{document}(M~s(x),B~s(x)) is the positive solution of (18) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[-L, L]$$\end{document}[-L,L] replaced by [g(s), h(s)]. This, combined with Proposition 2.5 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(g(s), h(s))\rightarrow (-\infty ,\infty )$$\end{document}(g(s),h(s))→(-∞,∞) as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}s→∞, shows that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&B^* \le \liminf _{t\rightarrow \infty } B(t+s,x)=\liminf _{t\rightarrow \infty } B(t,x) & \mathrm{locally\ uniformly\ in}\ {\mathbb R},\\&M^*\le \liminf _{t\rightarrow \infty } M(t+s,x)=\liminf _{t\rightarrow \infty } M(t,x) & \mathrm{locally\ uniformly\ in}\ {\mathbb R}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}B∗≤lim inft→∞B(t+s,x)=lim inft→∞B(t,x)locallyuniformlyinR,M∗≤lim inft→∞M(t+s,x)=lim inft→∞M(t,x)locallyuniformlyinR,which together with (36) proves (35). \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Proof of Theorem 1.2

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_\infty -g_\infty <\infty $$\end{document}h∞-g∞<∞, then (30) holds by Lemma 4.1-(i). Therefore, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-g_\infty =h_\infty =\infty $$\end{document}-g∞=h∞=∞ and (35) holds by Lemma 4.8. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□
